Found the data. Registry line 4183 says:
- `internal_death_rate: 0.343`
- `det_death_rate: 0`

So the registry confirms: internal DR = 34.3%, det DR = 0%. This matches the main text (experiments.tex:32).

But the appendix (main.tex line 479) says:
"internal strategy dies in 58.6% of episodes versus 24.3% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$)"

These appendix numbers are WRONG. The registry data is the ground truth:


- internal_death_rate: 0.343 (34.3%)
- det_death_rate: 0 (0%)
- death-rate differential: 34.3% - 0% = +34.3%

The appendix incorrectly states 58.6% vs 24.3%, which is a critical error. The main text is correct, but I should verify this against the raw episode data to be sure.

**Det deaths (score = -100):**
- chemistry-mix: 4
- freeze: 3
- grow-fruit: 0
- identify-life-stages-1: 4
- inclined-plane: 0
- lifespan-longest: 2
- measure-melting-pt: 4
Total det deaths: 4+3+0+4+0+2+4 = 17 out of 70 = 24.3%

Looking at the overlap, there are cases where both types of failures occurred in the same task, with chemistry-mix, freeze, and identify-life-stages-1 showing the most instances of both happening together. Looking at the complete breakdown, both alive accounts for 29 cases, which combined with the 17 both-dead cases gives 46 no-preference outcomes. The actual death rates from the raw data are internal at 58.6% and deterministic at 24.3%, a differential of 34.3%—which matches the appendix but contradicts the main text claiming internal was 34.3% versus deterministic at 0%, and the registry also shows this discrepancy with those same incorrect values.

Now I'm noticing another inconsistency: when I recount the both_dead cases across all tasks, I get 17, but the registry lists both_dead as 13. Let me verify this against the grep results to see where the mismatch is coming from.

Looking at the actual numbers from the raw data, the pattern is clear: 41 internal deaths out of 70 gives 58.6%, and 17 deterministic deaths out of 70 gives 24.3%, with a differential of 34.3%. The appendix has these calculations right, but the main text is conflating the differential with the internal death rate itself—claiming 34.3% internal versus 0% deterministic, when really the internal rate is 58.6% and deterministic is 24.3%. The registry values are also off across the board: the internal death rate should be 0.586 not 0.343, deterministic should be 0.243 not 0, and both_dead should be 17 not 13. It seems like the Worker may have recorded these metrics at a different time or used a different calculation method.

This is a significant discrepancy that needs fixing. The main text in experiments.tex needs to be updated to match what the appendix and raw data actually show, and I should verify whether the introduction has similar issues. The core issue is that experiments.tex line 32 reports the wrong absolute death rates for Llama—it should show 58.6% internal and 24.3% deterministic instead of 34.3% and 0%. The registry metrics need updating to match (0.586 and 0.243), while the appendix already has the correct values. I should also verify the Qwen differential claim of +26.5% in the introduction, which appears to be a rounding of 26.6%. The no-preference count breaks down correctly: 17 from both-dead cases and 29 from ties, totaling 46, so the label counts are accurate despite the registry's incorrect death rate fields. I'm confident in the data now, though flagging the analysis script issue is the Worker's responsibility. Let me check if the Threshold Routing appendix is actually referenced in the main text.

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

29	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
30	\label{sec:cross-model}
31	
32	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $58.6\%$ vs.\ deterministic $24.3\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\times}$ scaling results (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) and Reflexion experiments confirm that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
33

```text
476	\section{Cross-Model Verification}
477	\label{app:cross-model}
478	
479	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.5\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
480	
481	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
482	\label{app:failure-modes}
483	
484	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
485	
```

The model is Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct with seed=42 and max_steps=30. Since westb-13313 has exactly 2 RTX 5090 GPUs, cuda:0 and cuda:1 are the only available devices on this server.

The task distribution checks out at 11 total tasks, and the model configuration is correct. The job has launched successfully and is running. I'm noticing a discrepancy in the episode count though — the ROADMAP specifies 11 tasks × 20 episodes = 220 total, whereas the original Qwen experiment had 245 episodes because find-non-living-thing used 40 episodes instead of 20. For this Llama replication, sticking with 20 per task seems reasonable given the pilot only ran 70 episodes across 7 tasks. Let me verify the full task list is accounted for.
```

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"#!/bin/bash\\nset -euo pipefail\\n\\n# D118: Llama-3.1-8B full cross-model replication — GPU 0 (6 tasks)\\n# 6 tasks × 20 episodes × 2 conditions = 240 episode runs\\n# Each episode pair ~4.7 min → ~9.4 hours estimated\\n\\nsource /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base\\ncd /root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing\\n\\nexport SLIM_EPISODES=1\\nexport CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0\\n\\nMODEL_PATH=\\\"/root/autodl-tmp/models/LLM-Research/Meta-Llama-3___1-8B-Instruct\\\"\\nOUTPUT_DIR=\\\"results/llama_cross_model_full\\\"\\nGPU_ID=0\\nEPISODES=20\\nLOG_DIR=\\\"${OUTPUT_DIR}/logs\\\"\\nmkdir -p \\\"$LOG_DIR\\\"\\n\\nTASKS=(\\n  \\\"chemistry-mix\\\"\\n  \\\"find-animal\\\"\\n  \\\"find-non-living-thing\\\"\\n  \\\"freeze\\\"\\n  \\\"grow-fruit\\\"\\n  \\\"identify-life-stages-1\\\"\\n)\\n\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\necho \\\"D118: Llama-3.1-8B Full Cross-Model — GPU 0\\\"\\necho \\\"Tasks: ${#TASKS[@]}\\\"\\necho \\\"Episodes per task: $EPISODES\\\"\\necho \\\"Model: $MODEL_PATH\\\"\\necho \\\"Output: $OUTPUT_DIR\\\"\\necho \\\"GPU: $GPU_ID (CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES)\\\"\\necho \\\"Start: $(date)\\\"\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\n\\nTOTAL_START=$SECONDS\\nFAILED=0\\nCOMPLETED=0\\n\\nfor TASK in \\\"${TASKS[@]}\\\"; do\\n  TASK_START=$SECONDS\\n  TASK_LOG=\\\"${LOG_DIR}/${TASK}_gpu0.log\\\"\\n  echo \\\"\\\"\\n  echo \\\"--- [$TASK] Starting at $(date) ---\\\"\\n\\n  if python src/episode_level_collection.py \\\\\\n    --task_types \\\"$TASK\\\" \\\\\\n    --episodes \\\"$EPISODES\\\" \\\\\\n    --gpu_id \\\"$GPU_ID\\\" \\\\\\n    --model_path \\\"$MODEL_PATH\\\" \\\\\\n    --output_dir \\\"$OUTPUT_DIR\\\" \\\\\\n    --start_episode 0 \\\\\\n    --seed 42 \\\\\\n    --max_steps 30 \\\\\\n    2>&1 | tee \\\"$TASK_LOG\\\"; then\\n    ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TASK_START ))\\n    echo \\\"--- [$TASK] Done in ${ELAPSED}s ($(( ELAPSED / 60 )) min) ---\\\"\\n    COMPLETED=$((COMPLETED + 1))\\n  else\\n    ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TASK_START ))\\n    echo \\\"--- [$TASK] FAILED after ${ELAPSED}s ---\\\"\\n    FAILED=$((FAILED + 1))\\n  fi\\ndone\\n\\nTOTAL_ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TOTAL_START ))\\necho \\\"\\\"\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\necho \\\"GPU 0 complete at $(date)\\\"\\necho \\\"Total time: ${TOTAL_ELAPSED}s (~$((TOTAL_ELAPSED / 60)) min)\\\"\\necho \\\"Completed: $COMPLETED / ${#TASKS[@]}\\\"\\necho \\\"Failed: $FAILED / ${#TASKS[@]}\\\"\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\n\\necho \\\"\\\"\\necho \\\"=== PER-TASK DEATH SUMMARY (GPU 0) ===\\\"\\nfor TASK in \\\"${TASKS[@]}\\\"; do\\n  SUMMARY_FILE=\\\"${OUTPUT_DIR}/${TASK}/episode_summary.jsonl\\\"\\n  if [ -f \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" ]; then\\n    TOTAL=$(wc -l < \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\")\\n    INT_DEATHS=$(grep -c '\\\"internal_final\\\": -100' \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" || true)\\n    DET_DEATHS=$(grep -c '\\\"det_final\\\": -100' \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" || true)\\n    echo \\\"  $TASK: total=$TOTAL, int_deaths=$INT_DEATHS, det_deaths=$DET_DEATHS\\\"\\n  else\\n    echo \\\"  $TASK: NO DATA\\\"\\n  fi\\ndone\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.73}}"}

do\\n  TASK_START=$SECONDS\\n  TASK_LOG=\\\"${LOG_DIR}/${TASK}_gpu0.log\\\"\\n  echo \\\"\\\"\\n  echo \\\"--- [$TASK] Starting at $(date) ---\\\"\\n\\n  if python src/episode_level_collection.py \\\\\\n    --task_types \\\"$TASK\\\" \\\\\\n    --episodes \\\"$EPISODES\\\" \\\\\\n    --gpu_id \\\"$GPU_ID\\\" \\\\\\n    --model_path \\\"$MODEL_PATH\\\" \\\\\\n    --output_dir \\\"$OUTPUT_DIR\\\" \\\\\\n    --start_episode 0 \\\\\\n    --seed 42 \\\\\\n    --max_steps 30 \\\\\\n    2>&1 | tee \\\"$TASK_LOG\\\"; then\\n    ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TASK_START ))\\n    echo \\\"--- [$TASK] Done in ${ELAPSED}s ($(( ELAPSED / 60 )) min) ---\\\"\\n    COMPLETED=$((COMPLETED + 1))\\n  else\\n    ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TASK_START ))\\n    echo \\\"--- [$TASK] FAILED after ${ELAPSED}s ---\\\"\\n    FAILED=$((FAILED + 1))\\n  fi\\ndone\\n\\nTOTAL_ELAPSED=$(( SECONDS - TOTAL_START ))\\necho \\\"\\\"\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\necho \\\"GPU 0 complete at $(date)\\\"\\necho \\\"Total time: ${TOTAL_ELAPSED}s (~$((TOTAL_ELAPSED / 60)) min)\\\"\\necho \\\"Completed: $COMPLETED / ${#TASKS[@]}\\\"\\necho \\\"Failed: $FAILED / ${#TASKS[@]}\\\"\\necho \\\"==========================================\\\"\\n\\necho \\\"\\\"\\necho \\\"=== PER-TASK DEATH SUMMARY (GPU 0) ===\\\"\\nfor TASK in \\\"${TASKS[@]}\\\"; do\\n  SUMMARY_FILE=\\\"${OUTPUT_DIR}/${TASK}/episode_summary.jsonl\\\"\\n  if [ -f \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" ]; then\\n    TOTAL=$(wc -l < \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\")\\n    INT_DEATHS=$(grep -c '\\\"internal_final\\\": -100' \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" || true)\\n    DET_DEATHS=$(grep -c '\\\"det_final\\\": -100' \\\"$SUMMARY_FILE\\\" || true)\\n    echo \\\"  $TASK: total=$TOTAL, int_deaths=$INT_DEATHS, det_deaths=$DET_DEATHS\\\"\\n  else\\n    echo \\\"  $TASK: NO DATA\\\"\\n  fi\\ndone\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.73}}"

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a comprehensive review request from the Director. Let me systematically go through each dimension. I need to re-read the compressed paper carefully and think critically about each point.

Let me start by organizing my analysis:

1. **Narrative polish**: I've read all sections. Need to assess story flow post-compression.
2. **Llama full results contingency**: Think through both scenarios.
3. **28 negative results**: Need to check what these are in the registry.
4. **Most likely rejection reasons**: Think as an EMNLP reviewer.
5. **GPU utilization**: Assess whether 16% compute with 85.9% memory is normal for this workload.


6. **Narrative flow**: The abstract answers four key questions concisely. The introduction follows a clear arc from problem to gap to method to results. The method section is tightened—problem setup is now a single paragraph with essential formulas preserved and the algorithm deferred to the appendix. Experiments underwent major restructuring with Table 1 fixed and cross-environment results consolidated. Related work is organized into five thematic paragraphs, and the discussion spans seven sections.

7. **Structural concerns**: The problem setup in §2.1 feels rushed—the jump from formulation to data collection is abrupt. The cross-environment section references appendix tables and figures, which disrupts reading flow since readers expect to see them inline. The discussion is dense across its seven paragraphs with potentially choppy transitions. The oracle degeneracy paragraph is only two sentences and risks feeling disconnected.

8. **Strengths in framing**: The abstract is solid with concrete numbers (78.9%, 90.4%, p=0.81) and answers all four questions. The introduction avoids generic LLM praise and opens with specificity.

9. **Potential issue with number density**: The introduction packs many percentages (78.9%, 90.4%, 96.3%, 97.4%) which could overwhelm readers, though for a diagnostic paper this density might be justified.

10. **Llama replication scenarios**: If the full results align with the pilot, I can upgrade the claim from directional consistency to confirmed cross-model generalization, report the full N=220 across both 7B-class models, and argue that death dominance is model-independent. If results diverge partially, I'll need to qualify which tasks show reversed patterns and adjust the generalization claim accordingly.

11. **Handling the 28 negative results**: The registry contains numerous failed experiments—router training across multiple seeds and scales, unsuccessful retry controls, reflexion baselines, death-risk routing analysis, and data scaling attempts—which I'll need to contextualize as boundary conditions rather than failures.

12. **Underutilized negative findings**: The classification head degeneration, keyword-based routing's task-identity leakage (62.9%), the flat performance curve across data scaling (25% to 800%), and the cross-task transfer failure at chance level (49.2%) all point to a coherent narrative: routing signals aren't learnable at the step level because they're fundamentally task-type-level phenomena. The Discussion touches on this but could weave these threads together more tightly to strengthen the overall argument.

Now thinking about the most likely rejection angles from an EMNLP reviewer. The biggest vulnerability is probably the environment specificity — those core numbers (78.9%, DAF 90.4%) are entirely from ScienceWorld, where the -100 death penalty creates an unusually stark binary outcome. MATH gives us nulls (which is actually informative) and ALFWorld shows a different mechanism entirely, but a skeptical reviewer might argue this is too environment-dependent to claim broad insights. The current limitations section acknowledges this, and we have cross-environment probes and the Llama pilot, but I should reframe the contribution more explicitly as the framework itself rather than the specific quantitative findings. The novelty question is also looming — I need to think through what exactly is novel here beyond the empirical observations. The GPU utilization pattern makes sense given the workload — vLLM pre-allocates memory to 85.9% as configured, but compute stays low at 16% because each environment step alternates between brief GPU inference calls and longer CPU-bound environment interaction, leaving the GPU idle most of the time.

This is fundamentally different from continuous training or batch inference workloads. Batching across multiple parallel episodes could theoretically improve GPU utilization, but that would require significant pipeline restructuring and isn't worth the effort for a one-off 220-episode collection run, especially since the ~8-9 hour total runtime across 2 GPUs is already acceptable for the deadline.

Now I'm composing a comprehensive response and verifying some of the claims by reviewing the relevant sections.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NLbHTYhD5qnMaCbH6R7cZq`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (43.5\%), while $10{\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\sim}$35\%, yet none closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

Now I have a complete picture. Let me compose the comprehensive review. I have all 28 negative experiments in detail and all the paper sections.

Let me organize my response carefully for each of the 5 dimensions.

### 1. Narrative polish

Post-compression story flow assessment:

**Abstract**: Strong. Hits all 4 questions. Concrete numbers. One concern: doesn't mention the framework's cross-environment diagnostic capability as prominently as it could. The "MATH ($N=795$), no routing signal emerges" is good but could be punchier.

**Introduction**: Good 5-paragraph flow. The opening is specific (Qwen2.5-7B on ScienceWorld). 

The logical progression from existing routing assumptions through the gap in prior work to our diagnostic framework feels natural. The contributions are clearly laid out in three distinct points.

However, the Introduction is quite dense with statistics—percentages, p-values, and performance deltas scattered throughout. In a two-column format, this creates visual clutter. The transition between discussing cross-context checks and the next section feels slightly abrupt, leaving a small gap in the narrative flow.

Moving into the Experiments section, the internal structure is mostly solid: the Death Avoidance decomposition is well-grounded, the sensitivity analysis paragraph is packed with numbers though, and the Multi-Model Validation section is appropriately concise. The Agent Improvement results with Table 1 and dose-response patterns work well, but then the Cross-Environment section breaks the flow by referencing appendix materials. The Baselines and Router Training sections maintain coherence despite compression.

The Discussion has seven paragraph blocks, though the Oracle degeneracy section is quite brief at just two sentences.

Looking at the compression trade-offs: moving the cross-environment table and figure to the appendix disrupts readability, the sensitivity analysis became too dense when condensed, and that Oracle degeneracy paragraph feels underdeveloped as a standalone point.

For the Llama contingency, the pilot data shows a striking pattern—58.6% versus 24.3% with zero instances of the intermediate preference across 70 episodes. If the full 220-episode replication maintains this consistency or strengthens it, that would support upgrading the preliminary findings to a more robust claim.

The concrete updates would involve expanding the multi-model validation section from 7 tasks to the full 11 tasks, adding the Llama-specific death attribution frequency, and shifting the framing from preliminary pilot to full cross-model replication. I can also check whether the same anomalous zero-death task persists in Llama and compute task-level correlations between the two model families to demonstrate structural alignment.

If some tasks do show inconsistent patterns between models, I can reframe this as revealing model-specific vulnerability profiles—where death dominance holds universally but the particular tasks triggering it vary by architecture. This actually strengthens the framework by establishing boundary conditions rather than weakening it.

Now I'm looking at how the 28 negative results cluster into meaningful failure modes. The router training experiments—spanning classification head degeneration, implicit routing approaches, and various balanced accuracy attempts—all point to a consistent problem: the routing mechanism itself struggles to learn meaningful task-dependent strategies, with most configurations bottoming out around 58% accuracy or worse.

What's particularly useful here is that each failure type eliminates a different hypothesis about what might fix the router. The progression from classification approaches through implicit methods to generative routing and scaling to 4B shows the problem isn't just about architecture or scale—it's something more fundamental about the routing task. There's also the 0.5B backbone result showing an inverted U-shape pattern that hasn't been leveraged yet, which could strengthen the argument that router failure is structural rather than incidental. The cross-split robustness issue with all seeds performing below chance at the episode level is another underutilized piece that deserves more prominence in the narrative.

Looking at the intervention experiments, the threshold routing result is particularly striking—even with perfect knowledge of per-task death risk, you still can't beat the always-det baseline. That's a direct answer to whether routing has any value, but it's currently hidden in an unreferenced appendix. The retry and reflexion experiments show only marginal improvements, and the 0.5B inverted-U pattern could be mentioned more directly as evidence that the problem isn't about backbone capacity.

For transfer and generalization, the picture is consistent across all four experiments: cross-model label transfer degenerates to always-det, leave-one-out evaluation hits 50% accuracy (always-det optimal), cross-task transfer performs at chance, and majority voting defaults to always-det. The discussion covers LOTO and cross-task transfer, but the triangulation across these failures—showing that the router fundamentally can't generalize—deserves more emphasis.

On data scaling, I'm looking at three experiments showing flat performance from 25% to 800% data and consistent results across seeds. This is in the appendix and mentioned briefly, but it's a clean finding that deserves more prominence.

The biggest rejection risk is the single-environment limitation. My core results (78.9% accuracy, 90.4% DAF) all come from ScienceWorld with one specific penalty structure, which severely limits generalization claims.

MATH shows null results and ALFWorld shows information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure, so the framework itself might generalize but the specific findings don't. I've got some defenses—the limitations section is honest, cross-environment probes show the framework behaves differently in different contexts, Llama experiments show cross-model consistency, and I've done anti-cherry-picking analysis. But the real gap is that I don't have a second environment where DAF actually works well to validate the approach, and while I mention NetHack and Crafter as targets, I don't have data from them. I should also tighten my claims in the abstract and intro to avoid overclaiming.

The second risk is methodological simplicity. DAF is just δP / (δP + Δ_nd)—a derived quantity that's mathematically straightforward—and a reviewer could argue the framework amounts to running two experiments and comparing them.

The SCI is essentially a Gini coefficient, so the contribution looks more like an empirical observation than a methodological advance. I'm defending this by pointing out that no one's done this decomposition before, and the router training as a diagnostic probe adds some depth, plus the counterfactual episode pairing is a principled experimental design. But my methods section is compressed to about 1.5 pages, which makes the simplicity more obvious. I should emphasize that the *experimental design*—the pure-strategy counterfactual comparison—is where the methodological contribution lies, not in the formula itself.

The third risk is practical impact. Someone could ask: if death dominance means always use deterministic, why do we need a framework? I have a discussion section on scaling and practical implications that addresses this with a three-step approach.

The MATH null result actually validates the framework—it correctly predicts when there's no routing signal. The framework tells us when routing matters and when it doesn't. But the practical recommendation (identify catastrophic failure, estimate asymmetry, use deterministic if dominant) doesn't really require a formal framework. I should strengthen the argument that without this framework, practitioners might waste resources building learned routers in environments where routing value is degenerate.

On GPU utilization, I'm seeing 85.9% memory and 16% compute during vLLM inference with ScienceWorld rollout. This is expected because vLLM allocates most of that memory for the KV cache, and then each episode step creates a burst of GPU activity for inference followed by idle time while ScienceWorld runs on CPU.

With 30 steps per episode, about half the time is spent on CPU-bound environment interaction. Between episodes there's environment reset and data saving, all on CPU. Since I'm running episodes sequentially rather than batching them, there's only one inference happening at a time per GPU. The low compute utilization is just structural to interactive agent rollouts—the environment is the bottleneck. Batching parallel episodes would require significant code changes for marginal benefit, and the 8-9 hour runtime is acceptable anyway. The GPU memory is fully utilized, which is what matters for vLLM, so no optimization is needed.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017SXjSXExFWVQDAHk9uH5KX`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (43.5\%), while $10{\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\sim}$35\%, yet none closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A MATH evaluation ($N{=}795$, $p = 0.81$) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure. The framework reveals when learned routers add value over a fixed strategy and when catastrophic failure makes routing degenerate.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

### Tool result

_source line 2526 · 2026-05-19T03:36:52.106Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015JR4tNTbFWBVajLNqTTDqG`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\%, 95\% CI $[42.4\%, 55.9\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	Four intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($34.5\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). All four leave a ${\sim}$13--27~pp residual gap. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[28.0\%, 41.0\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared structural bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. Prior tool-routing work \citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings; in multi-step environments, the death-rate differential ($\delta = 26.6$~pp) resists all four tested intervention axes. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); optimal routing is near-degenerate under death dominance.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\sim}5\%$ \texttt{focus on} rate in both). \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is thus a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\% \to 37.0\%$ via prompt warning; $\to 29.0\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Scaling and practical implications.}
14	Qwen2.5-72B reduces death-prone death rate to $34.5\%$ yet DAF remains $90.9\%$; the gap to deterministic persists at ${\sim}$18~pp. Below a capability threshold (${\sim}$0.5B), agents fail to reach dangerous states at all (13.3\% DR; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}), suggesting an inverted-U relationship between capability and death risk. For practitioners: (1)~identify catastrophic failure mechanisms, (2)~estimate death-rate asymmetry via a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot, (3)~if death-asymmetry labels dominate, a fixed deterministic strategy likely matches a learned router. Our router training consumed ${\sim}$200 GPU-hours across 28 configurations without exceeding always-deterministic; the diagnostic framework could have predicted this outcome from a 50-episode pilot. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) validates this diagnostic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Framework applicability.}
17	The penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.
18	
```

_source line 2527 · 2026-05-19T03:36:51.863Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CMbf7qmK9de78EwugTto28`</summary>

```text
23	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}
24	Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0\,pp (range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}). Cluster-robust inference, wild cluster bootstrap ($p < 0.001$), 28-task directional consistency (100\%), and 8-seed cross-validation all confirm significance (Appendices~\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\ref{app:seed-robustness}).
25	
26	\paragraph{Seed robustness.}
27	A full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis, with virtually identical $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ ($2.83$ vs.\ $2.84$) and per-task correlation $r = 0.94$ (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).
```

</details>

70	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
71	\label{sec:math-contrast}
72	\label{sec:cross-env}
73	
74	We evaluate two additional environments as boundary probes (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env-details}). ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment (oracle simulator, code interpreter, admissible-action access), so results probe framework applicability rather than replicate findings.
75	
76	\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp), with balanced labels (107 vs.\ 95, 74.6\% no-preference). Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.
77	
78	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is $27.4\%$ vs.\ $3.0\%$ (35 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred, 98 no-preference). The deterministic advantage stems from this information gap, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.
79	
80	\subsection{Baselines}
81	\label{sec:baselines}
82	
83	\begin{table}[t]
84	\centering

### Tool result

_source line 2533 · 2026-05-19T03:36:57.447Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SmVWdiBWmMEJS9Mn4qiHKw`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	Concurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.
10	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
11	But no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.
12	
13	We propose a \emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\delta{=}0.266$ and $P{=}100$.
14	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% of ScienceWorld steps produce identical scores regardless of modality; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes.
18	
19	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
20	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
21	Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
22	Agent improvement baselines confirm that death dominance is robust: retry yields only marginal reduction, while $10{\times}$ model scaling (72B) and Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} each reduce death rates to ${\sim}$35\% through independent mechanisms, yet neither closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
23	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
24	
25	Our contributions are:
26	\begin{enumerate}
27	    \item \textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics and router training as a diagnostic probe (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}, \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
28	
29	    \item \textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\% of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$); the oracle degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\% overlap). Four intervention axes (retry, scaling, Reflexion, action-space filtering) all fail to close the gap (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
30	
31	    \item \textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($p = 0.81$) confirms the predicted null; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry; a Llama pilot shows cross-model consistency (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
32	\end{enumerate}
33	
34	
35	
```

Now let me verify the changes against my 5-point checklist:

### 1. Abstract new ending
The abstract now ends with: "The framework reveals when learned routers add value over a fixed strategy and when catastrophic failure makes routing degenerate."

This is better than the old ending (which ended on the MATH null result). It gives a meta-level takeaway about the framework's purpose. However, I notice the sentence structure could be slightly improved — "add value over a fixed strategy" and "makes routing degenerate" are two different outcomes, which is good for communicating the framework's discriminative power.

### 2. §3.1 sensitivity analysis compression


The sensitivity analysis now compresses multiple robustness checks into a tighter narrative: bootstrap confidence intervals confirm the DAF estimate is stable around 81-99%, per-task analysis shows positive effects across nearly all task types, and leave-one-task-out validation demonstrates the result isn't driven by any single outlier. I'm also noting that cluster-robust inference, wild bootstrap testing, and cross-validation all converge on significance, which strengthens the overall claim. The threshold routing result fits well into the Non-triviality section as evidence that even with perfect information, the oracle can't escape the constraints of death dominance. I'm noticing the reference might be incomplete though—let me check if that's causing any issues with the flow. The reference to `\ref{app:cross-model}` looks correct for the 0.5B inverted-U observation, though I should verify whether that experiment actually lives in the cross-model appendix or if it belongs in the ablation section instead. Let me trace back through the registry to confirm the 0.5B scaling result is properly housed where the citation points. The 200 GPU-hours claim is well-positioned in the Scaling section—it follows the practitioner guidance and connects the framework's cost-saving potential to concrete validation on MATH, making it the most compelling of the three additions for addressing practical impact concerns. I should verify the page count impact since the Discussion now has six merged paragraph blocks with additional content about threshold routing, the 0.5B result, and GPU costs, so I need to check if this expansion fits within constraints. The cross-env section got condensed by moving references to the appendix instead of keeping them in the main text, saving a few words. Sensitivity analysis trimmed nicely, but Discussion expanded with new content about threshold routing and GPU hours, roughly offsetting those gains. The abstract's final sentence stayed about the same length. Overall the paper should hold at around 8 pages, though it's getting tight.

Now I'm tracing through the narrative arc to make sure it flows logically. The abstract sets up the problem, method, key finding, and takeaway cleanly. Introduction moves from the specific Qwen case to broader context about signal diversity, then positions the framework and results. Methods section covers the three main components in order. Experiments section walks through the decomposition analysis, then per-task correlations, signal concentration patterns, sensitivity checks, seed robustness, and multi-model validation.

I notice the δ value in the introduction got corrected from 0.265 to 0.266, which matches the actual calculation of 42.9% minus 16.3%. That's accurate now. But I'm seeing the Discussion section got cut off mid-sentence at line 8. When I calculate using the more precise fractions, I get δ = 0.2653, which explains the rounding discrepancy across the paper—Introduction rounds to 0.266, experiments rounds down to 0.265, and Discussion reports 26.6pp, all stemming from how 0.2653 gets rounded at different decimal places.

Looking back at the actual text, I see the Introduction states 0.266 while experiments.tex has 0.265, with Discussion aligning to the Introduction's 0.266. This is a minor but noticeable inconsistency that should be unified across the document. I also notice the cross-environment section reference has been properly updated to point to the appendix instead of the non-existent Table 14.

_source line 2546 · 2026-05-19T03:40:28.440Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am operating as an independent scientific reviewer for the "simgate-modular-routing" project (EMNLP 2026 via ARR, DDL May 25, 2026). This session continued from two prior context windows. My role is to independently verify experiment data, cross-validate numbers, check GPU compliance, identify statistical issues and overclaims, and post findings to Director via `reviewer_post_to_director`. All output must be in Chinese.

   Specific tasks completed this session:
   - Complete 7-point post-compression verification (Paper Agent compressed ~10.5→8 pages)
   - Independently discover and verify CRITICAL Llama death rate data inconsistency
   - Review ROADMAP updates (D119 compression, D118 Llama expansion, M008 Llama data fix)
   - Verify D118 Llama full replication launch (westb-13313 dual GPU)
   - Sprint-to-accept system assessment (5 dimensions: narrative, Llama contingency, negative results, rejection risks, GPU utilization)
   - **Currently in progress**: Verify Paper Agent's narrative polish (6 modifications, 5 verification items)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Death Rate (DR)**: Core metric, `is_death = (score <= -100)`. Qwen baseline = 48.0% (10 death-prone tasks)
   - **DAF (Death Avoidance Factor)**: 90.4% penalty-dependent, 78.9% penalty-independent (label fraction)
   - **Five intervention gradient**: Baseline 48.0% → Retry 43.5% → Prompt 37.0% → 72B 34.5% / Reflexion 34.0% → Filter 29.0% → Deterministic 16.3%

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\\%, 95\\% CI $[42.4\\%, 55.9\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nFour intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\\%): Retry ($43.5\\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($34.5\\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). All four leave a ${\\sim}$13--27~pp residual gap. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\\%, 40.8\\%]$ and $[28.0\\%, 41.0\\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared structural bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings; in multi-step environments, the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) resists all four tested intervention axes. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); optimal routing is near-degenerate under death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is thus a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 37.0\\%$ via prompt warning; $\\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \\texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling and practical implications.}\nQwen2.5-72B reduces death-prone death rate to $34.5\\%$ yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$; the gap to deterministic persists at ${\\sim}$18~pp. Below a capability threshold (${\\sim}$0.5B), agents fail to reach dangerous states at all (13.3\\% DR; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}), suggesting an inverted-U relationship between capability and death risk. For practitioners: (1)~identify catastrophic failure mechanisms, (2)~estimate death-rate asymmetry via a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot, (3)~if death-asymmetry labels dominate, a fixed deterministic strategy likely matches a learned router. Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours across 28 configurations without exceeding always-deterministic; the diagnostic framework could have predicted this outcome from a 50-episode pilot. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) validates this diagnostic.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross 245 episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at the default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Given observed $\\delta = 0.266$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}) = 26.6 / 29.44 \\approx 90.4\\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are known, while the 78.9\\% label fraction is the penalty-independent empirical finding. Of these 75, 54 (72.0\\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0\\,pp (range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}). Cluster-robust inference, wild cluster bootstrap ($p < 0.001$), 28-task directional consistency (100\\%), and 8-seed cross-validation all confirm significance (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}\nA full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis, with virtually identical $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ ($2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$) and per-task correlation $r = 0.94$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $58.6\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $24.3\\%$ (cf.\\ Qwen: $42.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\\times}$ scaling results (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) and Reflexion experiments confirm that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ scale) & 34.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\\% death at all scales). All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) reduces death-prone rate to $34.5\\%$ with task-level variation from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Recomputing DAF with 72B yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to 7B ($90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp over five trials). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) then plateaus ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). Retry's per-trial conditional death rate stays constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$; Reflexion's declines to ${\\sim}31\\%$ by Trial~3 (per-task breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate as a function of trial count ($N$) for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Retry (blue) is nearly flat; Reflexion (red) drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus. Horizontal lines: 72B scaling baseline ($34.5\\%$) and deterministic baseline ($16.3\\%$). Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe evaluate two additional environments as boundary probes (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-env-details}). ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment (oracle simulator, code interpreter, admissible-action access), so results probe framework applicability rather than replicate findings.\n\n\\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp), with balanced labels (107 vs.\\ 95, 74.6\\% no-preference). Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is $27.4\\%$ vs.\\ $3.0\\%$ (35 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred, 98 no-preference). The deterministic advantage stems from this information gap, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \\emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).\n",
        "numLines": 111,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 111
      }

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (43.5\\%), while $10{\\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\\sim}$35\\%, yet none closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A MATH evaluation ($N{=}795$, $p = 0.81$) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure. The framework reveals when learned routers add value over a fixed strategy and when catastrophic failure makes routing degenerate.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }

{
  "parentUuid": "dd6d49a3-11d1-4535-96b8-b73175f978dc",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nConcurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.266$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% of ScienceWorld steps produce identical scores regardless of modality; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nAgent improvement baselines confirm that death dominance is robust: retry yields only marginal reduction, while $10{\\times}$ model scaling (72B) and Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} each reduce death rates to ${\\sim}$35\\% through independent mechanisms, yet neither closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics and router training as a diagnostic probe (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}, \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\\% of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$); the oracle degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\\% overlap). Four intervention axes (retry, scaling, Reflexion, action-space filtering) all fail to close the gap (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($p = 0.81$) confirms the predicted null; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry; a Llama pilot shows cross-model consistency (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 35,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 35
      }
    },
